# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/qcom,wcnss-pil.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/qcom,wcnss-pil.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm WCNSS Peripheral Image Loader` remoteproc controller binding. This document defines the binding for a component that loads and boots firmware on the Qualcomm WCNSS core. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `oneOf` with 2 accepted compatible forms and covers 5 compatible tokens: `qcom,pronto-v1-pil`, `qcom,pronto-v2-pil`, `qcom,pronto-v3-pil`, `qcom,pronto`, `qcom,riva-pil`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `firmware-name`, `vddpx-supply`, `vddmx-supply`, `vddcx-supply`, `power-domains`, `power-domain-names`, `qcom,smem-states`, `qcom,smem-state-names`, `memory-region`, `smd-edge`, `iris`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 22 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `clock-names`, `clocks`, `compatible`, `interrupt-names`, `interrupts`, `iris`, `memory-region`, `power-domain-names`, `power-domains`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `smd-edge`, `vddcx-supply`, `vdddig-supply`, `vddmx-supply`, `vddpa-supply`, `vddpx-supply`, `vddrfa-supply`, `vddxo-supply`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Remoteproc-facing properties define firmware loading and processor-control resources: address ranges `reg`, `reg-names`, memory-carveout links `reg`, `qcom,smem-states`, `qcom,smem-state-names`, `memory-region`, mailbox/interrupt/IPC hooks `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `firmware-name`, and clock/reset/power controls `vddpx-supply`, `vddmx-supply`, `vddcx-supply`, `power-domains`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `iris`, `vddpx-supply`, `memory-region`, `smd-edge`; these values are consumed by Linux remoteproc drivers before firmware boot, crash handling, and virtio/rpmsg setup.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, platform bus probing hands the validated node to a remoteproc driver, which maps resources, obtains clocks/resets/power domains, reserves memory, starts firmware, and may expose rpmsg/virtio endpoints. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/remoteproc/qcom,smd-edge.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `if`, `oneOf`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Bjorn Andersson <andersson@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/remoteproc/qcom,smd-edge.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 1 example block that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 16 top-level properties, 22 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/qcom,wcnss-pil.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/qcom,wcnss-pil.yaml` against representative DTS users using `qcom,pronto-v1-pil`, `qcom,pronto-v2-pil`, `qcom,pronto-v3-pil`, `qcom,pronto`, `qcom,riva-pil`. The file contains 1 inline example, so example compilation should be part of the signal. Integration signals include successful remoteproc probe, firmware load/start/stop logs, reserved-memory carveout lookup, mailbox/interrupt traffic, rpmsg endpoint creation where applicable, and clean recovery from firmware crash paths.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/qcom,wcnss-pil.yaml -->

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/renesas,rcar-rproc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/renesas,rcar-rproc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Renesas R-Car remote processor controller` remoteproc controller binding. This document defines the bindings for the remoteproc component that loads and boots firmwares on the Renesas R-Car family chipset. R-Car gen3 family may have a realtime processor, this processor shares peripheral and RAM with the host processor with the same address map. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `renesas,rcar-cr7`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `resets`, `power-domains`, `memory-region`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 4 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `compatible`, `memory-region`, `power-domains`, `resets`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Remoteproc-facing properties define firmware loading and processor-control resources: address ranges none, memory-carveout links `memory-region`, mailbox/interrupt/IPC hooks none, and clock/reset/power controls `resets`, `power-domains`. Required properties are `compatible`, `resets`, `memory-region`, `power-domains`; these values are consumed by Linux remoteproc drivers before firmware boot, crash handling, and virtio/rpmsg setup.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, platform bus probing hands the validated node to a remoteproc driver, which maps resources, obtains clocks/resets/power domains, reserves memory, starts firmware, and may expose rpmsg/virtio endpoints. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are none; schema composition/conditional keywords present are none.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/ti,k3-dsp-rproc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/ti,k3-dsp-rproc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `TI K3 DSP devices` remote processor subsystem binding. The TI K3 family of SoCs usually have one or more TI DSP Core sub-systems that are used to offload some of the processor-intensive tasks or algorithms, for achieving various system level goals. These processor sub-systems usually contain additional sub-modules like L1 and/or L2 caches/SRAMs, an Interrupt Controller, an external memory controller, a dedicated local power/sleep controller etc. The DSP processor cores in the K3 SoCs are usually either a TMS320C66x CorePac processor or a TMS320C71x CorePac processor. Each DSP Core sub-system is represented as a single DT node. Each node has a number of required or optional properties that enable the OS running on the host processor (Arm CorePac) to perform the device management of the remote processor... It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 4 compatible tokens: `ti,am62a-c7xv-dsp`, `ti,j721e-c66-dsp`, `ti,j721e-c71-dsp`, `ti,j721s2-c71-dsp`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `resets`, `firmware-name`, `mboxes`, `memory-region`, `sram`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 8 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `compatible`, `firmware-name`, `mboxes`, `memory-region`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `resets`, `ti,sci`, `ti,sci-dev-id`, `ti,sci-proc-ids`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Remoteproc-facing properties define firmware loading and processor-control resources: address ranges none, memory-carveout links `memory-region`, mailbox/interrupt/IPC hooks `firmware-name`, `mboxes`, `sram`, and clock/reset/power controls `resets`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `ti,sci`, `ti,sci-dev-id`, `ti,sci-proc-ids`, `resets`, `firmware-name`, `mboxes`, `memory-region`; these values are consumed by Linux remoteproc drivers before firmware boot, crash handling, and virtio/rpmsg setup.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, platform bus probing hands the validated node to a remoteproc driver, which maps resources, obtains clocks/resets/power domains, reserves memory, starts firmware, and may expose rpmsg/virtio endpoints. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/arm/keystone/ti,k3-sci-common.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Suman Anna <s-anna@ti.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/arm/keystone/ti,k3-sci-common.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 1 example block that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 6 top-level properties, 8 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

At runtime, platform bus probing hands the validated node to a remoteproc driver, which maps resources, obtains clocks/resets/power domains, reserves memory, starts firmware, and may expose rpmsg/virtio endpoints. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/ti,k3-r5f-rproc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/remoteproc/ti,k3-r5f-rproc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `TI K3 R5F processor subsystems` remote processor subsystem binding. The TI K3 family of SoCs usually have one or more dual-core Arm Cortex R5F processor subsystems/clusters (R5FSS). The dual core cluster can be used either in a LockStep mode providing safety/fault tolerance features or in a Split mode providing two individual compute cores for doubling the compute capacity on most SoCs. These are used together with other processors present on the SoC to achieve various system level goals. AM64x SoCs do not support LockStep mode, but rather a new non-safety mode called "Single-CPU" mode, where only Core0 is used, but with ability to use Core1's TCMs as well. AM62 SoC family support a single R5F core only which runs Device Manager firmware and can also be used as a remote processor with IPC communication. Each Dual-... It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

Top-level properties are `$nodename`, `compatible`, `power-domains`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `ranges`, `ti,cluster-mode`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 18 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `compatible`, `firmware-name`, `memory-region`, `memory-region-names`, `power-domains`, `ranges`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `resets`, `ti,sci`, `ti,sci-dev-id`, `ti,sci-proc-ids`. Node naming is constrained by {'pattern': '^r5fss(@.*)?'}. Remoteproc-facing properties define firmware loading and processor-control resources: address ranges `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `ranges`, memory-carveout links `ranges`, mailbox/interrupt/IPC hooks none, and clock/reset/power controls `power-domains`. Required properties are `compatible`, `power-domains`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `ranges`; these values are consumed by Linux remoteproc drivers before firmware boot, crash handling, and virtio/rpmsg setup.

The K3 SoCs containing ICSSG v1.0 (eg: AM65x SR1.0) also have two Auxiliary PRU cores called RTUs with slightly different IP integration. The K3 SoCs containing the revised ICSSG v1.1 (eg: J721E, AM65x SR2.0) have an extra two auxiliary Transmit PRU cores called Tx_PRUs that augment the PRUs. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 2 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `compatible`, `no-map`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Reserved-memory properties describe physical address ranges and firmware-visible buffers rather than driver methods. Addressing and placement keys are `reg`, with provider-specific metadata none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `no-map`; the ABI controls whether Linux maps, preserves, exposes, or hands the region to another subsystem.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, early devicetree reserved-memory parsing claims the region before ordinary memory management; later drivers or firmware interfaces look it up by compatible string or phandle. ## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reserved-memory/qcom,cmd-db.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Qualcomm Command DB` firmware/secure-world reserved-memory binding. Command DB is a database that provides a mapping between resource key and the resource address for a system resource managed by a remote processor. The data is stored in a shared memory region and is loaded by the remote processor. Some of the Qualcomm Technologies Inc SoC's have hardware accelerators for controlling shared resources. Depending on the board configuration the shared resource properties may change. These properties are dynamically probed by the remote processor and made available in the shared memory. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reserved-memory/ramoops.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reserved-memory/ramoops.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Ramoops oops/panic logger` persistent crash-log reserved-memory binding. ramoops provides persistent RAM storage for oops and panics, so they can be recovered after a reboot. This is a child-node of "/reserved-memory", and is named "ramoops" after the backend, rather than "pstore" which is the subsystem. Parts of this storage may be set aside for other persistent log buffers, such as kernel log messages, or for optional ECC error-correction data. The total size of these optional buffers must fit in the reserved region. Any remaining space will be used for a circular buffer of oops and panic records. These records have a configurable size, with a size of 0 indicating that they should be disabled. At least one of "record-size", "console-size", "ftrace-size", or "pmsg-size" must be set non-zero, but are otherwise optional... It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `ramoops`. Addressing and placement keys are `reg`, `mem-type`, with provider-specific metadata none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`; the ABI controls whether Linux maps, preserves, exposes, or hands the region to another subsystem.

Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `reserved-memory.yaml`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `anyOf`, `oneOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points

Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 2 compatible tokens: `allwinner,sun6i-a31-ahb1-reset`, `allwinner,sun6i-a31-clock-reset`. Top-level properties are `#reset-cells`, `compatible`, `reg`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `#reset-cells`, `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Reset-controller properties publish reset lines or reset-adjacent control registers to consumers: register resources `reg`, reset provider cells and names `#reset-cells`, and clock/power hooks `#reset-cells`. Required properties are `#reset-cells`, `compatible`, `reg`; consumer phandles depend on the cell count and identifier headers remaining stable.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, reset-controller drivers register reset lines with the Linux reset framework, while consumers assert or deassert those lines through phandle specifiers validated by this schema. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are none; schema composition/conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Chen-Yu Tsai <wens@csie.org>, Maxime Ripard <mripard@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are none; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 2 example blocks that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 3 top-level properties, 3 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/allwinner,sun6i-a31-clock-reset.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/allwinner,sun6i-a31-clock-reset.yaml` against representative DTS users using `allwinner,sun6i-a31-ahb1-reset`, `allwinner,sun6i-a31-clock-reset`. The file contains 2 inline examples, so example compilation should be part of the signal. Integration signals include reset-controller registration, consumer phandle validation, SoC DTSI builds with reset ID headers, and device probe paths that can assert/deassert the documented reset lines.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/fsl,imx-src.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/fsl,imx-src.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Freescale i.MX System Reset Controller` reset controller binding. The system reset controller can be used to reset the GPU, VPU, IPU, and OpenVG IP modules on i.MX5 and i.MX6 ICs. Those device nodes should specify the reset line on the SRC in their resets property, containing a phandle to the SRC device node and a RESET_INDEX specifying which module to reset, as described in reset.txt The following RESET_INDEX values are valid for i.MX5: GPU_RESET 0 VPU_RESET 1 IPU1_RESET 2 OPEN_VG_RESET 3 The following additional RESET_INDEX value is valid for i.MX6: IPU2_RESET 4 It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `oneOf` with 2 accepted compatible forms and covers 8 compatible tokens: `fsl,imx51-src`, `fsl,imx50-src`, `fsl,imx53-src`, `fsl,imx6q-src`, `fsl,imx6sx-src`, `fsl,imx6sl-src`, `fsl,imx6ul-src`, `fsl,imx6sll-src`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `#reset-cells`. ## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/renesas,rst.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Renesas R-Car and RZ/G Reset Controller` reset controller binding. The R-Car and RZ/G Reset Controllers provide reset control, and implement the following functions: - Latching of the levels on mode pins when PRESET# is negated, - Mode monitoring register, - Reset control of peripheral devices (on R-Car Gen1), - Watchdog timer (on R-Car Gen1), - Register-based reset control and boot address registers for the various CPU cores (on R-Car Gen2 and Gen3, and on RZ/G). It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/snps,axs10x-reset.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `AXS10x reset controller` reset controller binding. This binding describes the ARC AXS10x boards custom IP-block which allows to control reset signals of selected peripherals. For example DW GMAC, etc... This block is controlled via memory-mapped register (AKA CREG) which represents up-to 32 reset lines. As of today only the following lines are used: - DW GMAC - line 5 It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/sophgo,sg2042-reset.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Sophgo SG2042 SoC Reset Controller` reset controller binding. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `oneOf` with 2 accepted compatible forms and covers 3 compatible tokens: `sophgo,sg2044-reset`, `sophgo,sg2042-reset`, `sophgo,cv1800b-reset`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `#reset-cells`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `#reset-cells`, `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. Reset-controller properties publish reset lines or reset-adjacent control registers to consumers: register resources `reg`, reset provider cells and names `#reset-cells`, and clock/power hooks `#reset-cells`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `#reset-cells`; consumer phandles depend on the cell count and identifier headers remaining stable.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, reset-controller drivers register reset lines with the Linux reset framework, while consumers assert or deassert those lines through phandle specifiers validated by this schema. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are none; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `oneOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Chen Wang <unicorn_wang@outlook.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are none; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 1 example block that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 3 top-level properties, 3 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/sophgo,sg2042-reset.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/sophgo,sg2042-reset.yaml` against representative DTS users using `sophgo,sg2044-reset`, `sophgo,sg2042-reset`, `sophgo,cv1800b-reset`. The file contains 1 inline example, so example compilation should be part of the signal. Integration signals include reset-controller registration, consumer phandle validation, SoC DTSI builds with reset ID headers, and device probe paths that can assert/deassert the documented reset lines.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/sophgo,sg2042-reset.yaml -->

`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/st,stih407-picophyreset.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `STMicroelectronics STi family Sysconfig Picophy SoftReset Controller` reset controller binding. This binding describes a reset controller device that is used to enable and disable on-chip PicoPHY USB2 phy(s) using "softreset" control bits found in the STi family SoC system configuration registers. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/ti,sci-reset.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/ti,sci-reset.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `TI-SCI reset controller` reset controller binding. Some TI SoCs contain a system controller (like the Power Management Micro Controller (PMMC) on Keystone 66AK2G SoC) that are responsible for controlling the state of the various hardware modules present on the SoC. Communication between the host processor running an OS and the system controller happens through a protocol called TI System Control Interface (TI-SCI protocol). This reset controller node uses the TI SCI protocol to perform the reset management of various hardware modules present on the SoC. Must be a child node of the associated TI-SCI system controller node. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `ti,sci-reset`. Top-level properties are `$nodename`, `compatible`, `#reset-cells`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include none. Node naming is constrained by {'pattern': '^reset-controller$'}. Reset-controller properties publish reset lines or reset-adjacent control registers to consumers: register resources none, reset provider cells and names `#reset-cells`, and clock/power hooks `#reset-cells`. Required properties are none; consumer phandles depend on the cell count and identifier headers remaining stable.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are none; schema composition/conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Nishanth Menon <nm@ti.com>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are none; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 3 top-level properties, 3 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/ti,tps380x-reset.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/reset/ti,tps380x-reset.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `TI TPS380x reset controller` reset controller binding. The TPS380x family [1] of supervisory circuits monitor supply voltages to provide circuit initialization and timing supervision. The devices assert a RESET signal if the voltage drops below a preset threshold or upon a manual reset input (MR). The RESET output remains asserted for the factory programmed delay after the voltage return above its threshold or after the manual reset input is released. [1] https://www.ti.com/product/TPS3801 It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 1 compatible token: `ti,tps3801`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reset-gpios`, `#reset-cells`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `#reset-cells`, `compatible`, `reset-gpios`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/cpus.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/cpus.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `RISC-V CPUs` RISC-V CPU node binding. This document uses some terminology common to the RISC-V community that is not widely used, the definitions of which are listed here: hart: A hardware execution context, which contains all the state mandated by the RISC-V ISA: a PC and some registers. This terminology is designed to disambiguate software's view of execution contexts from any particular microarchitectural implementation strategy. For example, an Intel laptop containing one socket with two cores, each of which has two hyperthreads, could be described as having four harts. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `oneOf` with 3 accepted compatible forms and covers 27 compatible tokens: `amd,mbv32`, `amd,mbv64`, `andestech,ax45mp`, `canaan,k210`, `nuclei,ux900`, `sifive,bullet0`, `sifive,e5`, `sifive,e7`, `sifive,e71`, `sifive,p550`, `sifive,rocket0`, `sifive,s7`, `sifive,u5`, `sifive,u54`, `sifive,u7`, `sifive,u74`, `sifive,u74-mc`, `spacemit,x100`, `spacemit,x60`, `thead,c906`, `thead,c908`, `thead,c910`, `thead,c920`, `riscv`, and 3 more. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `mmu-type`, `reg`, `riscv,cbom-block-size`, `riscv,cbop-block-size`, `riscv,cboz-block-size`, `thead,vlenb`, `cache-op-block-size`, `timebase-frequency`, `interrupt-controller`, `cpu-idle-states`, `capacity-dmips-mhz`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 12 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `interrupt-controller`, `riscv,isa`, `riscv,isa-base`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RISC-V properties capture CPU, ISA, cache, MMU, and platform-compatible contracts. Relevant RISC-V keys are `mmu-type`, `riscv,cbom-block-size`, `riscv,cbop-block-size`, `riscv,cboz-block-size`, `timebase-frequency`, addressing or child topology keys `reg`, and interrupt/timer integration `interrupt-controller`. Required properties are `interrupt-controller`; these fields shape CPU enumeration, ISA extension probing, and platform matching.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, early RISC-V platform and CPU discovery uses the compatible strings and CPU/ISA properties to select platform hooks, enumerate harts, configure timers, and expose ISA features. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/cpu.yaml#`, `/schemas/interrupt-controller/riscv,cpu-intc.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `extensions.yaml`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `anyOf`, `dependencies`, `if`, `oneOf`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Paul Walmsley <paul.walmsley@sifive.com>, Palmer Dabbelt <palmer@sifive.com>, Conor Dooley <conor@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/cpu.yaml#`, `/schemas/interrupt-controller/riscv,cpu-intc.yaml#`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `extensions.yaml`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 2 example blocks that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 12 top-level properties, 12 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/cpus.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/cpus.yaml` against representative DTS users using `amd,mbv32`, `amd,mbv64`, `andestech,ax45mp`, `canaan,k210`, `nuclei,ux900`, `sifive,bullet0`, `sifive,e5`, `sifive,e7`, and 19 more. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/extensions.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/extensions.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `RISC-V ISA extensions` RISC-V ISA extension binding. RISC-V has a large number of extensions, some of which are "standard" extensions, meaning they are ratified by RISC-V International, and others are "vendor" extensions. This document defines properties that indicate whether a hart supports a given extension. Once a standard extension has been ratified, no changes in behaviour can be made without the creation of a new extension. The properties for standard extensions therefore map to their originally ratified states, with the exception of the I, Zicntr & Zihpm extensions. See the "i" property for more information. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses no explicit compatible schema and covers 0 compatible tokens: no explicit compatible values. Top-level properties are `riscv,isa`, `riscv,isa-base`, `riscv,isa-extensions`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include none. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RISC-V properties capture CPU, ISA, cache, MMU, and platform-compatible contracts. Relevant RISC-V keys are `riscv,isa`, `riscv,isa-base`, `riscv,isa-extensions`, addressing or child topology keys none, and interrupt/timer integration none. Required properties are none; these fields shape CPU enumeration, ISA extension probing, and platform matching.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, early RISC-V platform and CPU discovery uses the compatible strings and CPU/ISA properties to select platform hooks, enumerate harts, configure timers, and expose ISA features. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string-array`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `anyOf`, `if`, `oneOf`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Paul Walmsley <paul.walmsley@sifive.com>, Palmer Dabbelt <palmer@sifive.com>, Conor Dooley <conor@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string-array`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 0 example blocks that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 3 top-level properties, 3 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are allowed. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/extensions.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/riscv/extensions.yaml` against representative DTS users using the accepted compatible forms. The file contains 0 inline examples, so example compilation should be part of the signal. Integration signals include platform match selection, hart enumeration, ISA extension exposure in `/proc/cpuinfo` or hwprobe paths, cache/MMU property parsing, and boot coverage on DTS files that use the platform compatible.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/brcm,bcm2835.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/brcm,bcm2835.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `BCM2835/6368 Random number generator` hardware random-number generator binding. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 4 compatible tokens: `brcm,bcm2835-rng`, `brcm,bcm-nsp-rng`, `brcm,bcm5301x-rng`, `brcm,bcm6368-rng`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `resets`, `reset-names`, `interrupts`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 7 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `clock-names`, `clocks`, `compatible`, `reg`, `reset-names`, `resets`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RNG properties declare entropy-provider resources: register ranges {join_code(groups['addressing'], 8)}, clock/reset/power controls `clocks`, `clock-names`, `resets`, `reset-names`, interrupts `interrupts`, and RNG tuning keys `reg`, `clocks`, `resets`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`; runtime consumers are hwrng/crypto drivers that turn these resources into entropy devices.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, hwrng or crypto drivers bind to the node, map registers, enable clocks or resets, and feed entropy to the kernel random subsystem. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are none; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `if`, `then`.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/ti,keystone-rng.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/ti,keystone-rng.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Keystone SoC Hardware Random Number Generator` hardware random-number generator binding. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `ti,keystone-rng`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `reg`, `ti,syscon-sa-cfg`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 5 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `clock-names`, `clocks`, `compatible`, `reg`, `ti,syscon-sa-cfg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RNG properties declare entropy-provider resources: register ranges {join_code(groups['addressing'], 8)}, clock/reset/power controls `clocks`, `clock-names`, interrupts none, and RNG tuning keys `clocks`, `reg`. Required properties are `compatible`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `reg`, `ti,syscon-sa-cfg`; runtime consumers are hwrng/crypto drivers that turn these resources into entropy devices.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, hwrng or crypto drivers bind to the node, map registers, enable clocks or resets, and feed entropy to the kernel random subsystem. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Nishanth Menon <nm@ti.com>, Santosh Shilimkar <ssantosh@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/phandle`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 1 example block that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 5 top-level properties, 5 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/ti,keystone-rng.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/ti,keystone-rng.yaml` against representative DTS users using `ti,keystone-rng`. The file contains 1 inline example, so example compilation should be part of the signal. Integration signals include hwrng device registration, entropy reads through `/dev/hwrng` or kernel hwrng tests, clock/reset enablement, and absence of probe-time register/IRQ failures.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rng/ti,keystone-rng.yaml -->

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/abracon,abx80x.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/abracon,abx80x.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Abracon ABX80X I2C ultra low power RTC/Alarm chip` real-time clock binding. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 10 compatible tokens: `abracon,abx80x`, `abracon,ab0801`, `abracon,ab0803`, `abracon,ab0804`, `abracon,ab0805`, `abracon,ab1801`, `abracon,ab1803`, `abracon,ab1804`, `abracon,ab1805`, `microcrystal,rv1805`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `abracon,tc-diode`, `abracon,tc-resistor`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 5 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RTC properties declare timekeeping hardware resources: bus/register addressing {join_code(groups['addressing'], 8)}, alarm interrupts and wakeup capability `interrupts`, clocks or supplies none, and RTC-specific behavior keys `interrupts`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`; the ABI drives RTC class-device registration, alarm support, and wake-from-suspend behavior.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, RTC drivers bind through I2C/SPI/platform buses, register an RTC class device, program alarms, and coordinate wakeup interrupts and clock inputs. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `rtc.yaml#`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`, `dependentRequired`, `if`, `then`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: linux-rtc@vger.kernel.org. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `rtc.yaml#`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 1 example block that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 5 top-level properties, 5 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: composition is closed with `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/abracon,abx80x.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/abracon,abx80x.yaml` against representative DTS users using `abracon,abx80x`, `abracon,ab0801`, `abracon,ab0803`, `abracon,ab0804`, `abracon,ab0805`, `abracon,ab1801`, `abracon,ab1803`, `abracon,ab1804`, and 2 more. The file contains 1 inline example, so example compilation should be part of the signal. Integration signals include RTC class-device registration, time read/write, alarm IRQ delivery, suspend wake tests, and bus probe coverage for required regulators, clocks, and address cells.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/amlogic,meson-vrtc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/amlogic,meson-vrtc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Amlogic Virtual RTC (VRTC)` real-time clock binding. This is a Linux interface to an RTC managed by firmware, hence it's virtual from a Linux perspective. The interface is 1 register where an alarm time (in seconds) is to be written. The alarm register is a simple scratch register shared between the application processors (AP) and the secure co-processor (SCP.) When the AP suspends, the SCP will use the value of this register to program an always-on timer before going sleep. When the timer expires, the SCP will wake up and will then wake the AP. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 1 compatible token: `amlogic,meson-vrtc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `reg`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 2 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `compatible`, `reg`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RTC properties declare timekeeping hardware resources: bus/register addressing {join_code(groups['addressing'], 8)}, alarm interrupts and wakeup capability none, clocks or supplies none, and RTC-specific behavior keys none. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`; the ABI drives RTC class-device registration, alarm support, and wake-from-suspend behavior.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, RTC drivers bind through I2C/SPI/platform buses, register an RTC class device, program alarms, and coordinate wakeup interrupts and clock inputs. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are `rtc.yaml#`; schema composition/conditional keywords present are `allOf`.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Neil Armstrong <neil.armstrong@linaro.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are `rtc.yaml#`; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 1 example block that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 2 top-level properties, 2 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

## Test Signals
Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/amlogic,meson-vrtc.yaml` for targeted schema and example validation, then `make dtbs_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/amlogic,meson-vrtc.yaml` against representative DTS users using `amlogic,meson-vrtc`. # sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/apple,smc-rtc.yaml

## Purpose
`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/apple,smc-rtc.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `Apple SMC RTC` real-time clock binding. Apple Silicon Macs (M1, etc.) have an RTC that is part of the PMU IC, but most of the PMU functionality is abstracted out by the SMC. An additional RTC offset stored inside NVMEM is required to compute the current date/time. It is not executable Ceph or kernel logic; it is a hardware-description ABI used by DTS authors, dt-schema, and Linux subsystem drivers so that board descriptions match what the driver will parse at probe or early boot.

## Important APIs, Types, and Functions
The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses single `const` and covers 1 compatible token: `apple,smc-rtc`. Top-level properties are `compatible`, `nvmem-cells`, `nvmem-cell-names`. Across nested schemas and child nodes this file mentions 3 distinct property names; required properties observed at all levels include `compatible`, `nvmem-cell-names`, `nvmem-cells`. Node naming is constrained by no explicit node-name constraint. RTC properties declare timekeeping hardware resources: bus/register addressing {join_code(groups['addressing'], 8)}, alarm interrupts and wakeup capability none, clocks or supplies none, and RTC-specific behavior keys none. Required properties are `compatible`, `nvmem-cells`, `nvmem-cell-names`; the ABI drives RTC class-device registration, alarm support, and wake-from-suspend behavior.

## Control Flow, State, and Persistence
Control flow is declarative JSON-schema evaluation. `dt-doc-validate`, `dt_binding_check`, and `dtbs_check` load the YAML, expand `$ref` links, match nodes by `compatible` or referenced common-schema use, enforce required properties, evaluate composition/conditional keywords, and validate inline DTS examples. At runtime, RTC drivers bind through I2C/SPI/platform buses, register an RTC class device, program alarms, and coordinate wakeup interrupts and clock inputs. The YAML itself stores no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data; persistence is the source-controlled devicetree ABI and the DTB blobs built from DTS. External references used in evaluation are none; schema composition/conditional keywords present are none.

## Dependencies and Integration Points
Maintainers: Sven Peter <sven@kernel.org>. Integration points include the Linux devicetree core meta-schema, any referenced common binding schemas, in-tree DTS/DTSI users under the Ceph-client kernel source, and driver `of_match_table` entries for the compatible strings. `$ref` dependencies are none; pattern-property child-node APIs are none. The file provides 0 example blocks that should stay aligned with the schema and driver expectations.

## Risks
Risks are ABI and integration risks. Changing compatible ordering, required keys, resource names, child-node patterns, cell counts, or strictness can reject existing DTS files or let invalid hardware descriptions reach runtime probe. This schema has 3 top-level properties, 3 distinct property names across nested schemas, and this strictness profile: unknown top-level properties are rejected. Conditional branches and shared `$ref` schemas should be checked against all in-tree users because schema-only edits can still break board builds or driver binding.

`sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/rtc/epson,rx8900.yaml` is a Linux devicetree YAML schema for the `EPSON RX8900 / Microcrystal RV8803 Real-Time Clock` real-time clock binding. The public API surface is the schema's accepted node shape. `compatible` uses `enum` list and covers 3 compatible tokens: `epson,rx8804`, `epson,rx8900`, `microcrystal,rv8803`.
